<!-- head-1: body_md -->
The formulation above encompasses several stochastic transportation problems of independent interest. We briefly discuss three examples that will also help place our results in context.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- notation--1: body_md -->
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item Throughout $T>0$ is fixed. For a nonnegative integer $\ell$, $\mathbb{R}^\ell$ is endowed with the Euclidean distance $d$, and $\lambda$ denotes the Lebesgue measure.
	$\mathbb{S}^\ell_+$ denotes the set of $\ell\times\ell$ symmetric positive semidefinite matrices.
	By ${\cal C}^\ell:=C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^\ell)$ we denote the space of continuous paths equipped with the sup norm.
	When $\ell=1$, we drop the superscript.
	$\nabla $ and $\nabla^2$ denote the gradient and Hessian operators, respectively, acting only on the space variable $x\in  \mathbb{R}^{\ell}$ of a smooth function $f:[0,T]\times \mathbb{R}^{\ell} \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$.
	For a Polish space $E$, $C_b(E)$ denotes the space of bounded continuous functions.
	                                                                                                                                                                         
	For $p\geq 1$, we let $C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^\ell)$ denote the space continuous function of a mosth $p$-growth and
\(
C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^\ell) \coloneq \{\varphi\in \mathbb{C}_p(\mathbb{R}^\ell):   \exists a, b \in \mathbb{R}: a \leq \varphi(y)  \leq  b( 1+\| y\|^p),\, \forall y\in \mathbb{R}^\ell\}.
\)
	
\item For a metric space $\Omega$ and ${\cal F}$ its Borel $\sigma$-field, ${\cal P}(\Omega)$ denotes the set of probability measures on $\Omega$ equipped with weak topology. 
For $\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}(\Omega)$ and a countably generated sub-$\sigma$-field ${\cal G}\subseteq{\cal F}$, $(\mathbb{P}^{{\cal G}}_\omega)_{\omega\in\Omega}$ denotes a family of regular conditional probability distribution (r.c.p.d.)~of $\mathbb{P}$ knowing ${\cal G}$.
Given $\mathbb{Q}\in{\cal P}(\Omega)$, we define the concatenation $\mathbb{Q}\otimes \mathbb{P}^{{\cal G}}_\cdot (A) \coloneqq \int_\Omega \mathbb{P}^{{\cal G}}_\omega (A) \mathbb{Q}({\mathrm{d}} \omega), A\in {\cal F}$.
	For $\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^\ell)$, ${\cal H}(\mathbb{Q}| \mathbb{P})$ denotes the relative entropy of $\mathbb{Q}$ with respect to $\mathbb{P}$, given by 
 $\mathbb{E}^\mathbb{Q}\big[ \log \frac{{\mathrm{d}} \mathbb{Q}}{{\mathrm{d}} \mathbb{P}}\big]$, if $\mathbb{Q}\ll\mathbb{P}$ and $\infty$ otherwise.
For $\mu\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^\ell )$, and $\varphi:\mathbb{R}^\ell \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$, we set $\langle \varphi,\mu\rangle \coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^\ell} \varphi (x)\mu({\mathrm{d}} x)$.
In particular, $M_p(\mu)\coloneqq \int_{\mathbb{R}^\ell}\|x\|^p\mu({\mathrm{d}} x)$ denotes the $p^{\mathrm{th}}$-moment of $\mu$.
$\Pi(\mu,\nu)$ denotes the set of all couplings $\pi\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^\ell\times \mathbb{R}^\ell)$ with marginals $\mu$ and $\nu$ and $(\pi_x)_{x\in X}$ denotes the {$\mu\text{--a.e.}$~unique} regular disintegration with respect to the first marginal.
For $p\geq 1$, ${\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^\ell)$ denotes the family of $\mu\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^\ell)$ with finite $p^{\mathrm{th}}$-moment endowed with the the $p$-Wasserstein distance
\(
W_p^p (\mu,\nu)\coloneqq \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\pi\in \Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^\ell\times \mathbb{R}^\ell} \|x-y\|^p  \pi({\mathrm{d}} x, {\mathrm{d}} y).
\)
         


\item 
                                  
$v:[0,T]\times{\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ belongs to
$\mathcal C^{1,2}_{\rm flat}$ if:
\begin{enumerate*}[label=$(\alph*)$]
\item $v$ is continuous and, for every $\mu \in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, $t\longmapsto v(t ,\mu)$ is differentiable and $\partial_tv$ is continuous on $[0,T]\times{\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)$;
\item there is a jointly continuous
$u:[0,T]\times{\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)\times\mathbb{R}^m\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ such that $x\longmapsto u(t,\mu)(x)$ is
$C^2$ for every $(t,\mu)\in [0,T]\times{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, $\nabla_xu$ and $\nabla^2_xu$ are jointly continuous, and for all
$\mu,\mu'\in{\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ we have
\(
v(t,\mu')-v(t,\mu)=\int_0^1\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}
u\big(t,\lambda\mu'+(1-\lambda)\mu\big)(x)\,(\mu'-\mu)({\mathrm{d}} x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda;
\)
\item $\nabla_x u$ and
$\nabla^2_x u$ are bounded.                                                 
\end{enumerate*}
                                                   
                                                   
                                                                       
                                                                   
                                                                     
                                                                       
      
                 
The function $u$ is unique up to an additive constant, and thus, we normalize it by
                                                              
$\int u(t,\mu)(x) \mu({\mathrm{d}} x)=0$.
We call $u$ the flat derivative of $v$, denoted by $\frac{\delta v}{\delta\mu}(t,\mu)(x) \coloneqq u(t,\mu)(x)$, or simply $\delta v$, see, e.g., {\rm \cite{carmona2018probabilisticI,carmona2018probabilisticII}} for further details and properties.

\item $v:[0,T]\times{\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ belongs to
$\mathcal C^{1,2,{\rm full} }_{\rm flat}$ if: 
\begin{enumerate*}[label=$(\alph*)$]
\item $v\in\mathcal C^{1,2}_{\rm flat}$ and, for every $(x,\mu) \in \mathbb{R}^m\times {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, $t\longmapsto \delta v(t ,\mu)(x)$ is differentiable and $\partial_t \delta v$ is continuous on $[0,T]\times \mathbb{R}^m\times {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$;
\item $\delta v(t,\mu)(y)$ is flat-differentiable, i.e.,
\(
\delta^2v(t,\mu)(y;x)\!\coloneqq \frac{\delta}{\delta\mu}\big(\delta u(t,\cdot)(y)\big)(\mu)(x)
\);
  
   
                                                                      
   
\item $(x,y)\mapsto \delta^2v(t,\mu)(y;x)$ is jointly continuous and $C^2$ in $y$ and in $x$ separately;
\item $\psi\in \{\nabla_y\delta^2 v,\nabla^2_y\delta^2 v, \nabla_x\delta^2 v, \nabla^2_x\delta^2 v\}$ is jointly continuous and bounded;                                                   
\item $\partial_t \delta v $, $\nabla_y \delta v$, $\Delta_y \delta v$ are flat-differentiable with flat derivatives $\partial_t\delta^2v$, $\nabla_y\delta^2v$, $\Delta_y\delta^2v$.
\end{enumerate*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end notation--1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def.controlrule.weak}
Let $p\geq 1$ and $\nu\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$. A weak control rule with initial condition $\nu$ consists of a tuple
\[
\gamma:=\big(\Omega^\gamma,{\cal F}^\gamma,\mathbb{F}^\gamma=({\cal F}^\gamma_t)_{t\in [0,T]},\mathbb{G}^\gamma=({\cal G}^\gamma_t)_{t\in [0,T]}, \mathbb{P}^\gamma, X^\gamma,W^\gamma,\mu^\gamma,  u^\gamma\big)
\]
satisfying,
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item \label{def.controlrule.weak.i} $(\Omega^\gamma,{\cal F}^\gamma,\mathbb{P}^\gamma)$ is a probability space and $\mathbb{F}^\gamma,\mathbb{G}^\gamma$ are filtrations satisfying,
 
                                                                
\begin{equation*}                 
 {\cal G}^\gamma_t\subseteq{\cal F}^\gamma_t,\; \mbox{and }\; \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma} [\mathbf 1_D\, |\,{\cal G}^\gamma_t] =\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma} [\mathbf 1_D\, |\,{\cal G}^\gamma_T],\; \mathbb{P}^\gamma\text{--a.s.} \mbox{ for all } D\in\mathcal F^\gamma_t\vee\sigma(W^\gamma),\ t\in[0,T];
\end{equation*}

\item \label{def.controlrule.weak.ii} $X^\gamma=(X^\gamma_t)_{t\in[0,T]}$ is an $\mathbb{R}^m$--valued, $\mathbb{F}^\gamma$--adapted continuous process, $u^\gamma=(u^\gamma_t)_{t\in[0,T]},\, u_t^\gamma=(\alpha^\gamma_t,\sigma^\gamma_t(\sigma^\gamma_t)^{\!\top}),                                                                                         
t\in [0,T]$ is an ${\cal U}$--valued, $\mathbb{F}^\gamma$--predictable process, and
\[
	\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma}\bigg [\sup_{t\in [0,T]} \|X^\gamma_t\|^p\bigg]+\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma}\bigg[\int_0^T  \| u_t^\gamma\|^p \,\mathrm{d} t\bigg]<+\infty;                                 
\]
\item \label{def.controlrule.weak.iii}
$W^\gamma=(W^\gamma_t)_{t\in[0,T]}$ is an $\mathbb{R}^d$--valued, $(\mathbb{F}^\gamma,\mathbb{P}^\gamma)$--Brownian motion, 
	${\cal F}^\gamma_0\vee\sigma(W^\gamma)$ is $\mathbb{P}^\gamma-$independent of ${\cal G}^\gamma_T$,
	 and $\mu^\gamma=(\mu^\gamma_t)_{t\in[0,T]}$ is a ${\cal P}({\cal C}^m)$--valued, $\mathbb{G}^\gamma$--predictable process such that
\[
	\mu^\gamma_t  	=
	{\cal L}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma}(X^\gamma_{t\wedge\cdot}\, |\,{\cal G}^\gamma_t\big),
	{\mathrm{d}} t\otimes{\mathrm{d}} \mathbb{P}^\gamma\text{--a.e.}\mbox{~on }[0,T]\times\Omega^\gamma;
\]

\item \label{def.controlrule.weak.iv} $ \mathbb{P}^\gamma\circ (X^\gamma_0)^{-1}=\nu$, and it holds that
\[
X^\gamma_t = X_0^\gamma + \int_0^t\big( \alpha_r^\gamma+b(r, X^\gamma,\mu^\gamma_r) \big) \,\mathrm{d} r + \int_0^t  \sigma_r^\gamma  \,\mathrm{d} W_r^\gamma ,\; t\in [0,T],\, \mathbb{P}^\gamma\text{--a.s.}
\]
\end{enumerate}
We let 
$$\Gamma(\nu):=\{\gamma: \gamma\text{ is a weak control rule with initial condition $\nu$} \}.$$
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def.msim}
Let $\mu,\nu\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$. We say $\mu \=c \nu \Longleftrightarrow  {\cal W}_c(\mu,\nu)=0$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{assump.costs}
We say $c:\mathbb{R}^m\times {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}_+$ satisfies {\rm \Cref{assump.costs}} with $p\geq1$ if:
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item \label{assump.costs.1} It is jointly l.s.c.~with respect to the product topology of $\mathbb{R}^m\times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ and $c(x,\delta_x)=0$.
                                                                                   
\item \label{assump.costs.2} The map $\rho\longmapsto c(x,\rho)$ is convex for any $x\in \mathbb{R}^m$ in the sense that
\[
c(x,\theta \rho_1+(1-\theta )\rho_2)\leq \theta  c(x,\rho_1)+(1-\theta  )c(x,\rho_2),\,  \rho_1,\rho_2\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m),\, \theta \in [0,1].
\]
\item \label{assump.costs.3}  There is $C>0$ such that
\[
c(x,\rho)\leq C\big(1+\|x\|^p +M_p(\rho) \big), \, (x,\rho)\in \mathbb{R}^m\times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m).
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:defcomments}
                                                                              
        
Under the weak formulation in {\rm \Cref{def.controlrule.weak}}, following {\rm \cite[Definition~2.1]{djete2019mckean}}, the coefficients are evaluated along a possibly random process $\mu^\gamma$.
	The special case in which $\mathbb{G}^\gamma$ is trivial is compatible with {\rm \Cref{def.controlrule.weak}}.
	In that scenario {\rm \Cref{def.controlrule.weak}\ref{def.controlrule.weak.i}} and the independence in {\rm \Cref{def.controlrule.weak}\ref{def.controlrule.weak.iii}} are vacuous and ${\cal L}(X^\gamma_{t\wedge\cdot}|{\cal G}^\gamma_t) = \mathbb{P}^\gamma\circ(X^\gamma_{t\wedge\cdot})^{-1}$.
	We refer to rules of this special form as deterministic-flow rules and write $\Gamma^{\rm det}(\nu_{0})$ for their collection and, analogously, we define $\Gamma_{\! c}^{\rm det}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$. With this, we may also consider the McKean-Vlasov transportation problem
	\begin{align}\label{eq:problem.det}
	V_c^{\rm det}(\nu_{0}, \nu_{T})\coloneqq
	\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\gamma \in \Gamma_{\! c}^{\rm det}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})} J(\gamma).
	\end{align}
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{assump.bf}
{Let $\nu_{0},\nu_{T}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$} and the functions $b$ and $f$ satisfy
	                                                            
	\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
	\item \label{assump.bf.i}  {For every $t \in [0,T]$ the function $({x}, {u}, \mu)\longmapsto f(t, x , u,\mu)$ is continuous, nonnegative and convex in u {\rm ;} and $f$ is uniformly continuous in $t$.
 That is,
 \begin{align*}
 	\Delta_tf(\varepsilon) := \sup \frac{|f(t, x,u,\mu) - f(s, x, u,\mu)|}{1 + f(s, x, u,\mu)} \longrightarrow 0, \mbox{ as }  \varepsilon\longrightarrow 0,  
 \end{align*}
where the supremum is taken over all $(t,s)\in [0,T]^2$, such that $|t - s|\le \varepsilon$ and $(x, u , \mu) \in  {\cal C}^{ m}\times {\cal U}\times {\cal P}({\cal C}^m)$;}

                                                                                                                                                      
                                                                
	\item \label{assump.bf.2} There is $\hat p > 1$ and $C_1 \in \mathbb{R}, C_2>0$, such that for all $(t, x, u , \mu) \in [0,T]\times {\cal C}^m \times {\cal U}\times {\cal P}({\cal C}^m)$
	\[
	    f(t,x,u,\mu) \geq C_1+C_2\| u\|^{\hat p};
	\]\vspace{-17pt}
	\item \label{assump.bf.3} The function $(t,x, \mu)\longmapsto b(t,x,\mu)$ is measurable and continuous in $\mu$, and 
                                                            
 there is $C>0$ such that for all $(t, x,\mu) \in [0,T]\times {\cal C}\times {\cal P}({\cal C}^m)$,
                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                                        
	\[
	\| b(t,x,\mu) \|\leq C\big (\|x\|+ M_2(\mu)^{1/2} \big).
	\]
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
{\rm Assumption \ref{assump.bf}} collects standard structural conditions ensuring compactness and stability of admissible weak control rules, while remaining flexible enough to accommodate McKean-Vlasov interactions. 
	The convexity of $f$, its continuity in $( x,u,\mu)$, together with its uniform continuity in time, are tailored to guarantee lower semicontinuity of the cost functional under weak convergence of controls and states, which is essential for the existence argument.
	The coercivity condition in {\rm$(ii)$} provides uniform integrability of the control processes and plays a key role in establishing tightness of admissible laws. 
	Finally, the linear growth condition on the drift $b$ in {\rm $(iii)$}, imposed only in terms of the current state and the marginal flow, is sufficient to ensure tightness of the state process.
	Notably, we do not impose global Lipschitz assumptions on $b$, as these are not needed for the variational analysis developed here;\footnote{Indeed, these are not necessary conditions for the existence of a weak control rule, see, for instance, \cite[Assumption S]{carmona2015probabilistic} or \cite[Assumption B2]{lacker2018strong}.}~instead, the formulation relies on weak solutions and martingale problem techniques, which allow for greater generality.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:existence}
	Let {\rm Assumptions} {\rm\ref{assump.costs}} and {\rm \ref{assump.bf}} hold and let $(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ be such that $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})<+\infty$.
    Then, there exists $\gamma \in {\Gamma_c}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ such that $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) = J(\gamma)$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk.exist.unique}
A key feature of this result is that no Lipschitz condition is imposed on the drift. The proof relies entirely on the compactness of controlled laws and the stability of the associated martingale problems, rather than on pathwise arguments.\medskip

Let us also remark that the uniqueness of the optimizer remains a much more subtle question. 
	In particular, in the above weak formulation, the problem may have multiple solutions even if we strengthen our assumptions on $b$. 
	In fact, even for specific instances of $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$, such as the mean field Schrödinger problem described in the introduction, uniqueness remains open in general, owing to the nonlinear nature of the underlying entropy minimization problem, see {\rm \cite[Remark 1.1]{backhoff2020mean}}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:duality.gen}
	If {\rm Assumptions }{\rm \ref{assump.costs}} and {\rm \ref{assump.bf}} are satisfied, then it holds
	\begin{equation*}
		V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) 
		= 
		\sup_{\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)}
		\big( \Psi^\varphi( \nu_{0})   - \langle \varphi,\nu_{T}\rangle 
		\big),
	\end{equation*}
	where for every $\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, the function $\Psi:{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ is given by
	\begin{equation}
	\label{eq:def.Phi}
		\Psi^\varphi(\nu) \coloneqq \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\gamma \in \Gamma(\nu)}J^\varphi(\gamma), \; \mbox{ where } J^\varphi(\gamma)\coloneqq \mathbb{E}^{ \mathbb{P}^\gamma}\bigg[\int_0^Tf(s, X^\gamma, u_s^\gamma, \mu_s^\gamma)\,\mathrm{d} s+ Q_c\varphi(X_T^\gamma) \bigg].
	\end{equation}
Moreover, we have that \(\Psi^\varphi(\nu) =\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\gamma \in \Gamma_{\rm det}(\nu)}J^\varphi(\gamma)\), and,
\begin{align*}
(V_c^{\rm det}(\nu_{0},\cdot)^\ast)^\ast=V_c(\nu_{0},\cdot).
\end{align*}	
                                                          
                 
                    
                                                                                                                              
                
                       
              
                  
                                                             
                                                
             
	                                                                                               
              
Thus, as a function of the terminal law, $V_c$ is the lower-semicontinuous convex envelop of $V_c^{\mathrm{det}}$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def.potential}
A Borel measurable function $\varphi:\mathbb{R}^m\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$  is a mean field potential whenever $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})=\Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})-\langle \varphi,\nu_{T}\rangle$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{theorem.verification}
Let {\rm \Cref{assump.bf}} hold.
Let $v$ be a solution to \eqref{eq:HJB} with datum $\varphi$. Moreover, let $\gamma^\star\in \Gamma(\nu_{0})$.\footnote{We denote the elements of $\gamma^\star$ with a $\star$ superscript.}
The following are equivalent
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item 
\(
H (t,X^{\star}_t,\mu^{\star}_t, \nabla \delta v (t,\mu^{\star}_t )(X^{\star}_t),  \nabla^2 \delta v (t,\mu^{\star}_t )(X^{\star}_t))= h (t,X^{\star}_t,\mu^{\star}_t, \nabla \delta v  (t,\mu^{\star}_t )(X^{\star}_t),  \nabla_x^2 \delta v (t,\mu^{\star}_t )(X^{\star}_t), u^{\star}_t), 
\; {\mathrm{d}} t\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{\star}\text{-a.e.}
\)
\item $\gamma^\star$ is optimal for $\Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})$ and $v(0,\nu_{0})=\Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})$.
\end{enumerate}
	Moreover, if $c \equiv \int_{\mathbb{R}^m} \1{x\neq y}\rho ({\mathrm{d}} y)$, then the following statements hold:
	\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\alph*)$, ref=.$(\alph*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
	\item Let $\varphi^\star$ be a mean field potential and $v^{\varphi^\star\!\!}$~be a solution to \eqref{eq:HJB}. 
	Let $\gamma^\star\in \Gamma_{\rm det} (\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ be such that $(i)$ holds, then $\gamma^\star$ is optimal for $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$. 
\item Let $\gamma^\star\in \Gamma_{\rm det} (\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ be optimal for $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$. 
	Let $\varphi^\star$ be such that $(i)$ holds with $v^{\varphi^\star\!\!}$ solution to \eqref{eq:HJB}, then $\varphi^\star$ is a mean field potential.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def.weak.N.sol}
Let $\nu_{0}\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, $N\geq 1$ be fixed.
	$\Gamma^N(\nu_{0}^{\otimes N})$ denotes the collection of $\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in {\rm Prob}(\Omega^N)$ for which there is a $\mathbb{R}^{Nm}$--valued, $\mathbb{F}^N$--predictable $\boldsymbol{\alpha} = (\alpha^1,\dots,\alpha^N)$ such that
\begin{equation*}
	\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}} \bigg[\int_0^T\|\sigma^\dagger \alpha^i_t\|^2\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg]<+\infty,                                          
\end{equation*}
                                                               
                  
                                                                                                                                           
                
and a $(\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\mathbb{F}^N)$--Brownian motion, $(W^{1,\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}},\dots,W^{N,\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}})$ such that
\begin{equation*}
                        
	X^{i,N}_t = 
	X_0^{i} 
	+ 
	\int_0^t\big( \alpha^i_s+ b( X^{i,N}_s, m^N_{\mathbb{X}_s})\big) \,\mathrm{d} s 
	+ 
	         
	\sigma        
	W^{i,\mathbb{P}^{N\!,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}_t,\; t\in [0,T], \;  \mathbb{P}^{N,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\text{--a.s.},\; i=1,\dots, N,\;  \mathbb{P}^{N,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\circ (\mathbb{X}_0)^{-1}=\nu_{0}^{\otimes N}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{assump.Df}
	{\rm \Cref{assump.Df}} is satisfied if $m=1$                  
	and it holds
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
	\item \label{assump.Df.1} {$\sigma\sigma^\top \ge \Lambda I_m$ for some $\Lambda>0$}.
	\item \label{assump.Df.2} {The function $b$ is time-independent, continuously differentiable and $\ell_b$--Lipschitz continuous with linear growth, with ${\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)$ equipped with the $2$-Wasserstein metric.
		                                                                
		Moreover, 
		                                                                                                                                                                
		the $\mathbb{R}^{m N}$-valued function ${\cal B}(\setBold[0.2] x \unsetBold) \coloneqq (b(x^1,m^N_{\setBold[0.2] x \unsetBold}),\dots, b(x^N, m^N_{\setBold[0.2] x \unsetBold}))^\top$ satisfies 
		                                                                                                                                       
		the dissipativity condition: there is $\rho\geq 0$ such that
			\[
			-\left\langle \Sigma^\top \xi,\,\Sigma^\top \nabla{\cal B}(\setBold[0.2] x \unsetBold)\xi \right\rangle
			\ge
			\rho\|\Sigma^\top\xi\|^2, \mbox{ for all }  \setBold[0.2] x \unsetBold,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{m N},
			\]
where $\Sigma\coloneqq I_N\otimes \sigma \in \mathbb{R}^{m N\times d N}$ denotes the Kronecker product of $I_N\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}$ with $\sigma\in \mathbb{R}^{m\timesd}$.}

                                            
   
             
                
  
                      
      
        
         
   
                       
   
                                      
                 
   
                      
   
                                          
                 
   
 
                                                                                                                               
   
                     
   
                                   
   
                   
    
   
                                    
                                                   
   
             
  
                   
    
                  
  
                           
  
	\item \label{assump.Df.3}                                                                                                  
		For any $N\geq 1$, the measure $\mathbb{P}^N$ is reversible. 
	\item \label{assump.Df.4} There is $c_0>0$ such that $\int_{\mathbb{R}^m}e^{c_0\|x\|^2}\nu_0({\mathrm{d}} x)<+\infty$.
	\item \label{assump.Df.5} $\nu_{T}$ satisfies Poincar\'e inequality, is equivalent to Lebesgue measure $\lambda$ and $w_T \coloneqq {\mathrm{d}}\nu_{T}/{\mathrm{d}}\lambda$ satisfies the lower-envelope condition: there is $C_\nu>0$ and $\kappa>0$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
	\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{y\in B_x}w_T(y) \ge C_{\nu} e^{-\kappa(1 + \|x\|^2)}, \mbox{ for all $x\in \mathbb{R}^m$,}
\end{equation*}
where $B_x$ is the ball centered at $x$ of radius $1$, and $\kappa <\min (c_0/c_1, (2T\lambda_{\rm max}(\sigma\sigma^\top) c_1)^{-1})$ with $c_1\coloneqq 2{\mathrm{e}}^{8\ell_b T^2}\big(1 + 8\ell_b T^2 e^{16\ell_b T^2} \big)$.
                                                                                               

                                                                                                                  
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
Let us comment on the previous conditions on the data.
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]	
\item The uniform ellipticity condition guarantees that the infinitesimal generator $L_N$ of the diffusion $\mathbb{X}$ defined by \eqref{eq.Np.sde.ref} is a strictly elliptic operator.
	This, together with the assumption on the dimension of the state process, will provide the regularizing effects that allow us to derive a key Sobolev compactness of a sequence of Schr\"odinger potentials in the proof of the convergence result.
	The assumption of constant $\sigma$ simplifies the analysis involving the associated carr\'e du champ operator, see {\rm \Cref{sec.upperbound}}.
	We expect that non-constant, uniformly elliptic $\sigma$ could also be treated, at the cost of substantial additional technicalities.
	We stress that the assumption on $m$ is needed only at one step in the argument, namely, {\rm \Cref{prop.conv.phi^N}}.
	This is the reason we keep the notation for the general case in the proof.
	See {\rm \Cref{remark.assump.dim.den}} for further comments on this.
	
\item In {\rm \Cref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.2}--\ref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.3}} we have in mind models of the form
\begin{equation*}
	b(x,\mu) = -\nabla F(x) - \int_{\mathbb{R}^m} \nabla_x G(x,y)\, \mu ({\mathrm{d}} y) ,
\end{equation*}
for a $C^2$ and convex confining potential $F$, and a $C^2$, bounded from below, symmetric and jointly convex interaction potential $G$ {and $ \sigma\sigma^\top=\Lambda I_m$}.
	For our purpose, in {\rm\Cref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.2}} it is enough to assume $\rho\ge0$.
	For the above form of $b$, if the (joint) convexity coefficient $\rho$ is strictly positive and $\sigma$ is time-independent, then {\rm \Cref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.2}--\ref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.3}} are already satisfied, see {\rm \cite{cattiaux2023time}}.
\item The reversibility assumption on $\mathbb{P}^N$ will be key to derive the regularity of a sequence of potentials of $V^N$.
	It follows from {\rm\Cref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.2}} that $\mathbb{P}^N$ is the unique weak solution to \eqref{eq.Np.sde.ref}.

                                                                               
                                                       
\item Assuming that $\nu_{T}$ satisfies Poincar\'e inequality can be seen as a regularity constraint on the target distribution. 
This condition is well known to be weaker than the log-concavity of $\nu_{T}$.

\item The conditions in {\rm \Cref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.4}--\ref{assump.Df}\ref{assump.Df.5}} are technical and required in our analysis.
	Given the controlled dynamics in \eqref{eq:N-part.schroedinger}, we believe the square exponential form of the lower envelope of the density $w_T$ is compatible with Gaussian-type tails.
	The bound on $\kappa$ is, ultimately, a restriction relating $T$ and $\ell_b$.
	This condition arises from the need to derive uniform exponential moment estimates in our proof of the upper bound.
	If one is willing to assume a stronger form of the lower envelope, this condition can be weakened or removed altogether.
	                                                                                                 
	Indeed, see {\rm \Cref{remark.assump.dim.den}} for other settings under which these assumptions can be weakened or dropped.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:parti.conv.N}
	Let Assumptions {\rm \ref{assump.bf}} and {\rm \ref{assump.Df}} be satisfied, and further assume that $\nu_{0}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R})$ for some $p>2$.
	Let $V_e$ and $V^N_e$ be respectively given by \eqref{eq:e.problem} and \eqref{eq:N-part.schroedinger}.
	If we have
	\begin{align}\label{assump.parti.conv.N}
	\sup_{N\ge1}N^{-1}V^N_e(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) <\infty,\mbox{ and, } \sup_{N\ge1}N^{-1} {\cal H}(\nu_{0}^{\otimes N}|\mu^N) <\infty,
	\end{align}
                                                                                     
	then it holds
	\begin{equation*}
		\lim_{N\to \infty}N^{-1}V^N_e(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) = V_{e}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}).
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:dual.exists.char}
	Under the conditions of {\rm \Cref{thm:parti.conv.N}}, there exists an optimal mean field Schr\"odinger potential.
	That is, there is a function $\varphi\in \mathbb{L}^2(\mathbb{R},\nu_{T})$ admitting a weak gradient $($in the sense of distributions$)$ in \(\mathbb{L}^2(\mathbb{R},\nu_{T})\)
	                                                                        
	such that 
	\[
	V_e(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) =   \Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0}) - \langle \varphi,\nu_{T}\rangle.
	\]
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{remark.assump.dim.den}
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item It is natural to ask whether the above potential is unique, at least up to an additive constant, as is the case in the classical, i.e., non mean field, Schrödinger problems.
Based on the discussion in {\rm \Cref{rmk.exist.unique}}, we leave this for future work.
                                                  
\item The growth requirement on the density of $w_T$ of $\nu_{T}$ and the condition $m=1$ are technical conditions that may be avoided using different proof techniques.
They are needed here only to be able to use Sobolev embedding with respect to Lebesgue measure (embed $H^1$ into $\mathbb{L}^\infty$), which allows to derive uniform in $N$ growth properties on Schr\"odinger potentials of the $N$ particle problem.
These conditions can be avoided for instance if we can, a priori, show that the $N$ particle problem admits optimal potentials $\varphi^{i,N}$ that have polynomial growth: $|\varphi^{i,N}(x)| \le C(1 + |x|^p)$ for some $p\ge0$.
Moreover, these conditions on the dimension as well as the tail estimates and lower-envelope assumptions on the target density, can be dropped if the problem is set on a torus as for instance in {\rm \citet*{berman2020sinkhorn,baradat2021regularized,lavenant2019new,yao2025convergence,soner2024viscosity}}. In this case, the state space would be compact and the growth properties needed on the Schr\"odinger potential would be easier to derive.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor.HJB.linearization}
	Let $V\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^m)$ be symmetric with bounded Hessian, $b(t,x,\mu)\equiv -\nabla V\ast \mu(x)$, $m=d$, and $\sigma=I$.
	Let $v\in {\cal C}^{1,2,{\rm full}}_{\rm flat}$ be a solution to \eqref{eq:HJB} with datum a mean field potential $\varphi^\star$, as constructed in {\rm \Cref{cor:dual.exists.char}}.
	Let $\gamma^\star\in \Gamma_{\rm det} (\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ be such that 
                                               
	$\alpha_t^{\star}=-\nabla \delta v (t,\mu_t^\star )( X^\star_t),  \; {\mathrm{d}} t\otimes {\mathrm{d}} \mathbb{P}^{\star}\text{--a.e.}$ 
	Then, there is                                                      
	$\psi^\star\in C^{1,2}([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^m)\cap  C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^m)$, satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BCGL17}
	\partial_t\psi^\star
	+
	\tfrac12\Delta\psi^\star
	+
	\tfrac12|\nabla\psi^\star |^2
	-
	 \int_{\mathbb{R}^m}
	 \nabla V(x-\tilde x)\cdot\big(\nabla\psi^\star(t,x)-\nabla\psi^\star(t,\tilde x)\big)
	 \,\mu^\star_t({\mathrm{d}}\tilde x)
	 =
	 0,\;
	\psi^\star(T,\cdot)=-\varphi^\star
            
\end{equation}
In particular, $\psi^\star (t,x)\coloneqq -  \delta v (t,\mu_t^\star )( x) +\int_t^T\varrho(s)\,{\mathrm{d}} s+\int\varphi^\star\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^\star_T$, for a function $\varrho:[0,T)\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$.\medskip
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item	It is not hard to show that $\mu^\star$ satisfies $\partial_t\mu^\star_t-\tfrac12\Delta\mu^\star_t+\nabla\cdot\big((-\nabla V\ast\mu^\star_t+\nabla\psi^\star)\mu^\star_t\big)=0$ in the weak sense.
	Consequently, the pair $(\psi^\star,\mu^\star)$ is a solution of {\rm \cite[Corollary 1.1]{backhoff2020mean}}, and moreover, $\psi^\star$ is a solution of the corresponding equation in {\rm \cite[Corollary 1.2]{backhoff2020mean}}, although with specified terminal condition $\psi^\star(T,\cdot)=-\varphi^\star$ where $\varphi^\star$ is a mean field potential for $V_e(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.

\item Let us also note that, in contrast to {\rm \Cref{theorem.verification}}, {\rm \Cref{cor.HJB.linearization}} imposes additional regularity on $v$.
	To see why, recall that the terminal condition of the {\rm HJB} equation \eqref{eq:HJB} is $\langle \varphi,\mu\rangle$, echoing the fact that the ambient domain of this equation is $[0,T] \times {\cal P}_2(\mathbb{R}^m)$.
	Consequently, arriving at \eqref{eq:BCGL17} requires additional regularity in the measure variable for $v$.
	This is precisely the idea behind {\rm \Cref{cor.HJB.linearization}}, which identifies \eqref{eq:HJB} with \eqref{eq:BCGL17} after \emph{linearizing}, i.e., taking an additional linear derivative, alongside the optimal flow $\mu^\star_\cdot$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def.controlrule}
Let $p\geq 1$ and $\nu\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$. We denote by ${\cal P}(\nu)$ the collection of $\mathbb{P}\in {\rm Prob}(\Omega)$ under which\footnote{Recall that by definition of $u$ it can be that $u_t\notin \pi(U)$ on a null set.
	The condition in $(i)$ and the introduction of the cemetery point $\iota$ guarantee $u_t$ is always well defined, even on that null set. Condition $(ii)$ should be understood in the sense of the associated martingale problem, see {\rm \cite{stroock1997multidimensional,djete2019mckean}}} 
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item \label{def.controlrule.1} $\mathbb{P}[u_t\in {\cal U}]=1$, for $ \lambda \text{--a.e.}\, t\in [0,T]$ and
\[
 \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P}\bigg[  \sup_{t\in [0,T]} \|X_t\|^p \bigg] +  \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P}\bigg[\int_0^T \|u_t\| ^p{\mathrm{d}} t \bigg]<\infty.                                   
\]
                                                                                                                         
\item \label{def.controlrule.2} For all $\phi \in C_b^2(\mathbb{R}^{m+d})$, the process $M^\phi: [0,T]\times \Omega \longrightarrow \mathbb{R} $ is an $(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$--local martingale, with $M^\phi$ defined by
\[
M_t^\phi\coloneqq \phi(X_t,W_t)-\int_0^t ({\cal L} \phi) (s,X,W, \mu_s,u_s)\,\mathrm{d} s,\, t\in [0,T].
\]
                                         
                                                                                                                                
                                                                                       
\item  \label{def.controlrule.3} $\mathbb{P}\circ X_0^{-1}=\nu$, $\mathbb{P}\circ W_0^{-1}=\delta_0$, the pair $(X_0,W)$ is $\mathbb{P}$-independent of $\overline{{\cal G}}_T$, and, for every
$t\in[0,T]$,
                                      
\[
	\bar\mu_t =
	\mathbb{P}^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}_{\omega}\circ (X_{t\wedge\cdot},U_{t\wedge\cdot},W)^{-1},
	\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}
\]

\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rmk.equiv} 
\begin{enumerate}[label=$(\roman*)$, ref=.$(\roman*)$,wide,  labelindent=0pt]
\item We remark that the sets $\Gamma(\nu_{0})$ and ${\cal P}(\nu_{0})$ are in correspondence, see {\rm \cite[Lemma 4.3]{djete2019mckean}}. 
	Indeed, for any $\gamma\in \Gamma(\nu_{0})$ we have that $\overline\mathbb{P}^\gamma:=\mathbb{P}^\gamma\circ(X^\gamma,U^\gamma,W^\gamma,\mu^\gamma)^{-1}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})$, with \( \widehat\mu^{\,\gamma}_t\coloneqq {\cal L}^{\mathbb{P}^\gamma}  ( (X^\gamma_{t\wedge\cdot},U^\gamma_{t\wedge\cdot},W^\gamma) \, |\,\mathcal G^\gamma_T ),\, t\in[0,T] \).
                                                  
Conversely, given $\overline\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})$, there exists a weak control rule $\gamma\in \Gamma(\nu_{0})$ such that $\mathbb{P}^\gamma\circ(X^\gamma,U^\gamma,W^\gamma,\mu^\gamma)^{-1}=\overline\mathbb{P}$. 
In particular, this shows that 
\[
J(\gamma)={\cal J}(\mathbb{P}).
\]
\item In complete analogy with \Cref{rem:defcomments}, we denote by ${\cal P}_{\rm det}(\nu)$ the collection of elements in ${\cal P}(\nu)$ with trivial $\bar \mathbb{G}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{Lem:v-LSC}
	Let {\rm Assumptions \ref{assump.costs} and \ref{assump.bf}} hold. The function ${\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)\times {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)\ni (\mu,\nu)\longmapsto V_c(\mu,\nu) \in \mathbb{R} \cup\{+\infty\}$ is lower semicontinuous for the topology of weak convergence.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The proof follows some ideas of the proof of \cite[Lemma 3.1]{mikami2008optimal}.
	We argue in three steps.\medskip
	
	{\bf Step 1.} Let $(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n)$ be a sequence of elements of ${\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)\times {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$ converging to $(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.
	Without loss of generality, we assume that $\liminf_{n\to \infty}V_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n) <\infty$.
	Thus, up to a subsequence, $(V_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n))_{n\in \mathbb{N}}$ is bounded.
	In particular, for each $n\ge1$ there is $\gamma_n\in \Gamma_{\! c}(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n)$ such that
	\begin{align}
	\label{eq:lsc.eps-optimal}
		V_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n) \ge J(\gamma_n) - \frac1n
	\end{align}
	and combined with the coercivity condition Assumption \ref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.2}, we have 
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
	\begin{equation*}
		\sup_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{\gamma_n}}\bigg[\int_0^T\|u^{\gamma_n}_s \|^{\hat p} \,\mathrm{d} s \bigg] <\infty.
	\end{equation*}
	Thus, on $(\Omega^{\gamma_n},{\cal F}^{\gamma_n},\mathbb{F}^{\gamma_n},\mathbb{P}^{\gamma_n})$, with $u^{\gamma_n}:= (\alpha^{\gamma_n},\sigma^{\gamma_n}{\sigma^{\gamma_n}}^\top)$, thanks to \Cref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.3}, we have that
	\begin{align}\label{eq.assZheng}
	X^{\gamma_n}_0,\; \int_0^T \| \alpha_s^{\gamma_n} + b(s, X^{\gamma_n}, \mu^{\gamma_n}_s)\|^{\hat p} \,\mathrm{d} s,\; \text{and,} \; 	\int_0^T \|\sigma^{\gamma_n}_s\sigma^{\gamma_n \top}_s\|^{\hat p} \,\mathrm{d} s,
	\end{align}
	are uniformly bounded in probability. 
	Recall \eqref{eq.repofcontrols} and note that thanks to \Cref{rmk.equiv}, we have that 
	\[
	\mathbb{P}^n\coloneqq \mathbb{P}^{\gamma^n}\circ(X^{\gamma^n}, U^{\gamma^n}, W^{\gamma^n}, \bar \mu^{\gamma^n})^{-1}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0}^n).
	\]
	Note that by definition $(\mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq 1}\subseteq {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n)$.\medskip
	
	{\bf Step 2.} We claim that $(\mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq 1}\subseteq {\rm Prob}(\Omega)$ is tight and that any of its limit laws is in ${\cal P}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.
	We first note that for all $n\geq 1$, $X= X_0+ A + M^n, \mathbb{P}^n\text{--a.s.}$, and $\langle X\rangle=\langle M^n\rangle=\Sigma$, $M^n$ denoting the $\mathbb{P}^n$-local martingale part,
	                                                  
	for absolutely continuous processes $A$ and $\Sigma$ on $\Omega$ defined by \(
A_\cdot \coloneqq \int_0^\cdot \big( \alpha_s + b(s, X,  \mu_s )\big) \,\mathrm{d} s, \; \Sigma_\cdot:=\int_0^\cdot \sigma_s \sigma^\top_s \,\mathrm{d} s.\)\medskip
                                                  
                                                                                                               
          
	
Notice now that
	\[
		\sup_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\bigg[\int_0^T\|u_s \|^{\hat p} \,\mathrm{d} s \bigg] = \sup_{n\in \mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{\gamma_n}}\bigg[\int_0^T\|u^{\gamma_n}_s \|^{\hat p} \,\mathrm{d} s \bigg] <\infty,
	\]
	                                                                                                                                                                                       
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
	and, thanks to \Cref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.3}, the analogous version of \eqref{eq.assZheng} holds on $\Omega$.
	By \cite[Theorem 3]{zheng1985tightness}, $\widehat \mathbb{P}^n:= \mathbb{P}^{n}\circ(X, A, M, \Sigma)^{-1}$, where $M=X-X_0-A$, is tight for the uniform topology on $\widehat\Omega:=({\cal C}^m)^3\times {\cal C}^{m\times m}$.\footnote{the process $(X, A, \Sigma, M)$ is tight on $(\widehat\Omega,\mathbb{P}^n)$.}		
	With this, we are ready to argue the claim. 
	First note that, by definition of $\mathbb{P}^n\in{\cal P}(\nu_0)$, $\mathbb{P}^n\circ W^{-1}$ is tight and $U_t=\int_0^t \pi(u_t)\,\mathrm{d} t, \mathbb{P}^n\text{--a.s.}$, and thus, $\mathbb{P}^n\circ U^{-1}$ is tight.
	Now note that, by the tower property and \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.3}, $\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^n}[\bar\mu_T]=\mathbb{P}^n\circ (X,U,W)^{-1}$, and thanks to \cite[Lemma A.2]{lacker2015mean}, $\mathbb{P}^n\circ \bar \mu_T^{-1}$ is tight in ${\cal P}({\cal P}(\bar\Omega))$.
	Thanks to \Cref{lem:flowdetermined}, we have that $\bar\mu=\mu_T\circ (\bar  X_{t\wedge \cdot},\bar  U_{t\wedge \cdot},\bar W)^{-1}$, $\mathbb{P}^n\text{--a.s.}$, for every $n$.
	Hence, $\mathbb{P}^n\circ \bar \mu^{-1}$ is tight in ${\cal P}(C[0,T],\mathbb{P}(\bar\Omega))$.
	Thanks to \cite[Lemma A.2]{lacker2015mean} again, we deduce that $(\mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq1}$ is tight.\medskip
	

	
Let $\mathbb{P}$ be a limit law of $(\mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq1}$. 
	Notice that $\mathbb{P}$ induces $\widehat\mathbb{P}\in {\rm Prob}(\widehat\Omega)$ which is a limit law of $(\widehat \mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq1}$.
	Letting $(\widehat X, \widehat A, \widehat M, \widehat \Sigma)$ denote the coordinate process on $\widehat\Omega$, \cite[Theorem 3]{zheng1985tightness} ensures that under $\widehat \mathbb{P}$, 
	                                                                                                 
	                                                                                                                             
	$\widehat X_t = \widehat X_0 + \widehat A_t + \widehat M_t, \widehat\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.},$ $\widehat M$ is a continuous local martingale, and, $\widehat A_t$ and $\widehat \Sigma_t: = \langle \widehat M\rangle_t$ are absolutely continuous, with densities $ (\hat a , \hat \beta)$ such that
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_0^T\big( \|\hat a_s\|^{\hat p}+\|\hat \beta_s\|^{\hat p}\big) \,\mathrm{d} s <\infty,\; \widehat  \mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}
	\end{equation*}
	
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           
	Let us now define $\hat \mu_t:= \widehat \mathbb{P}\circ (\widehat X_{t\wedge\cdot})^{-1}$, $\hat u_t:=(\hat \alpha_t,\hat \sigma_t)$, where $\hat \sigma  =\hat \beta^{1/2}$, $\hat \alpha_t:= \hat a_t - b(t, \widehat X,  \hat \mu_t )$ and note that $\hat u$ is in correspondence with the process $u$ defined on $\Omega$.
	Since ${\cal U}$ is closed we get from the Portmanteau theorem that $n \int_{( t-1/n)\vee 0}^t  u_s \,\mathrm{d} s \in {\cal U},  \mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$ for every $t\in [0,T]$ and $n\geq 1$.
	Thus, $ \mathbb{P}[  u_t \in {\cal U}]=1$ for $\lambda \text{--a.e.}\, t\in [0,T]$.
	Using \Cref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.3}, we deduce that $\mathbb{P}$ satisfies \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.1}.
	Moreover, it follows from Itô's formula that for any $\hat \phi \in C_b^2(\mathbb{R}^m)$, the process
	\[
	\widehat M_t^{\hat \phi}=\hat \phi(\widehat X_t)-\int_0^t \big(  ( \hat\alpha_s+b(s,\widehat X,\hat \mu_s) )\cdot \nabla \hat\phi (\widehat X_s)+\frac12 {\rm Tr}[\hat \beta_s \nabla^2\hat\phi(\widehat X_s)]\big) \,\mathrm{d} s, \; t\in [0,T],
	\]
	is an $(\widehat  \mathbb{F},\widehat\mathbb{P})$-martingale, where $\widehat\mathbb{F}$ denotes the filtration generated by $(\widehat X, \widehat A, \widehat M, \widehat \Sigma)$.
	By \cite[Theorem 4.2.1]{stroock1997multidimensional}, it follows that $ \widehat \mathbb{P}$ is a solution to the martingale problem associated with \eqref{eq.dyn} on $ \widehat\Omega$.
	Consequently, recall \Cref{rmk.equiv} and $\widehat \mathbb{P}=  \mathbb{P}\circ ( X, A, M,\Sigma)^{-1}$, $\mathbb{P}$ solves the martingale problem associated with \eqref{eq.dyn} on $\Omega$.
	As in the proof of \cite[Theorem 4.5.2]{stroock1997multidimensional}, we deduce that on $\Omega$, $W$ is a $(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$--Brownian motion and \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.2} holds.
	Thus, for any $\phi \in C_b^2(\mathbb{R}^{m+d})$ the process $M^\phi$ is an $(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$-local martingale. 
	That is, \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.2} holds.
	Verifying the independence of $(X_0,W)$ and $\bar{\cal G}_T$ as well as the identity in \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.3} follows as in \cite[Section 4.3.2.2]{djete2020mckean2}.

	\medskip
	
	Finally, we claim that $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
	         
	                                                                                                                                                                                       
	                                                     
	indeed, since $\mathbb{P}^{n} \circ (X)^{-1}\longrightarrow \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}$ and $\mathbb{P}^{n}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n)$, it follows on the one hand that $\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_0  = \nu_{0}$ and on the other hand, since by \cite[Theorem 2.9]{backhoff2019existence} the function ${\cal W}_c$ is lower semicontinuous, we have that
                                                                         
	\begin{equation*}
		0 = \liminf_{n \to \infty} {\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}^n\circ X^{-1}_T, \nu_{T}^n) \geq {\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T, \nu_{T}),
	\end{equation*}
	which shows that $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.
	                                                                                   

	\medskip

	{\bf Step 3.}         
	Let us now argue the statement of the Lemma. 
	Let $(\mathbb{P}^n)_{n\geq 1}\subseteq {\rm Prob}(\Omega)$ and $\mathbb{P}$ be as in the previous step. 
	Let us now show that $\liminf_{n\to \infty} {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n) \ge {\cal J}(\mathbb{P})$.
	Arguing as in the proof of \cite[inequality (3.17)]{mikami2002optimal}, we obtain from \Cref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.i} that
	\begin{align*}
		\int_0^Tf(s, {\bf x}, u_s, \mu) \,\mathrm{d} s \ge \frac{1}{1 + \Delta_tf(\varepsilon)}\bigg( \int_0^{T - \varepsilon} f(s,{\bf x}, \varepsilon^{-1}(\eta_{s+\varepsilon}-\eta_s), \mu )\,\mathrm{d} s - T \Delta_tf(\varepsilon)\bigg)
	\end{align*}
	for every $(t,{\bf x}, \mu)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb{R}^m\times{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, and $\mathbb{R}^m\times\mathbb{S}^{m}_+$-valued process $u$, with $\eta_t = \int_0^t u_s\,\mathrm{d} s $.
	                                                    
	                
	                                                                                                                                                                                                       
	              
	   
	Recall that for any $\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})$, $\mathbb{P}[u_t \in {\cal U}]=1$ for $\lambda \text{--a.e.}\,  t\in [0,T]$ and $\eta_\cdot \coloneqq \int_0^\cdot u_s\,\mathrm{d} s$ is well defined on $\Omega$.
	Thus, 
	                                                                                         
	since $\mathbb{P}^n\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})$, we have 
	                                                                       
	\begin{align*}
		{\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n) &\ge \frac{1}{1 + \Delta_tf(\varepsilon)}\bigg(  \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^n}\bigg[\int_0^{T - \varepsilon} f(s,X , \varepsilon^{-1}(\eta_{s+\varepsilon}-\eta_s) \big) , \mu ) \,\mathrm{d} s \bigg] - T \Delta_t f(\varepsilon)\bigg).
				                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
	\end{align*}
	                                                                                                                                                                                        
	Now, since $f$ is continuous and nonnegative, Fatou's Lemma gives that $(X,\eta,\mu)\longmapsto \int_0^{T-\varepsilon}  f(s,X , \varepsilon^{-1}(\eta_{s+\varepsilon}-\eta_s) \big) , \mu ) \,\mathrm{d} s $ is lower-semicontinuous, and thus, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\liminf_{n\longrightarrow\infty} {\cal J} (\mathbb{P}^n)  \ge \frac{1}{1 + \Delta_tf(\varepsilon)}\bigg(  \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\int_0^{T - \varepsilon} f(s,X,   \varepsilon^{-1}(\eta_{s+\varepsilon}-\eta_s)  ,\mu_s )ds \bigg] - T \Delta_tf(\varepsilon)\bigg)
 	\end{equation*}
	Now, taking the limit as $\varepsilon\longrightarrow 0$, which is justified again by Fatou's lemma, we have thanks to \Cref{assump.bf}\ref{assump.bf.i} that
 	\begin{equation*}
 		\liminf_{n\longrightarrow \infty}{\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n) \ge \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\int_0^Tf(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)ds\bigg] = {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}).
 	\end{equation*}
 	Using \eqref{eq:lsc.eps-optimal}, that $J(\gamma^n)={\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n)$, and $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}(\mu,\nu)$ yields
 	\begin{equation*}
 		\liminf_{n\longrightarrow \infty}V_c(\nu_{0}^n,\nu_{T}^n)\ge V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}),
 	\end{equation*}
 	from which we deduce lower semicontinuity of $V_c$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Since ${\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \neq \emptyset$, then for every $n\ge1$ there is $\mathbb{P}^n\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ such that
	\begin{equation*}
			V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \ge {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n) - \frac1n.
	\end{equation*}	
	As argued in the proof of Lemma \ref{Lem:v-LSC} above, there is $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ such that ${\cal J}(\mathbb{P}) \le \liminf_{n\to\infty}{\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^n)$,
	                                          
	                                 
	and, by \Cref{rmk.equiv}, there is a weak control rule $\gamma\in \Gamma(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ such that $J(\gamma)={\cal J}(\mathbb{P})$.
	Thus, $\gamma$ is optimal for Problem \eqref{eq:problem}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:noconvexdet}
	Let $m =d=1$, $\sigma\equiv1$, $\nu_{0}\coloneqq \mathcal N(0,1)$, $f\equiv  \tfrac12|a|^2$, \( b(t,x,\mu)\coloneqq \int_{\mathbb R}y\,\mu(t)(\mathrm dy)-x_t,\, c(x,\rho)\coloneqq \int_\mathbb{R} \1{x\neq y}\rho({\mathrm{d}} y)\).
	Moreover, let $ \upsilon >0$, $\theta^2>0$, and \( \nu_{T}^{+}\coloneqq \mathcal N( \upsilon,\theta^2),\; 
 	\nu_{T}^{-}\coloneqq \mathcal N(- \upsilon,\theta ^2),\;
	\bar{\nu}_T \coloneqq \tfrac12\nu_{T}^{+}+\tfrac12\nu_{T}^{-}\).
Then, {\rm Assumptions~A and~B} hold, and for a constant $c_{\theta,T}>0$ independent of $\upsilon$, we have that
\[
V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\nu_{T}^{+})\leq 
	\frac{\upsilon^2}{2T}+c_{\theta,T},\; 
	V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\nu_{T}^{-})
	\leq 
	\frac{\upsilon^2}{2T}+c_{\theta,T} ,\mbox{ and, } 
	V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\bar{\nu}_T) 
	\geq C_{\upsilon,\theta,T},
	\mbox{ where }
	C_{\upsilon,\theta,T}\coloneqq \frac{(\sqrt{\upsilon^2+\theta^2}-{\mathrm{e}}^{-T}-1/\sqrt2\big)_+^2}{1-{\mathrm{e}}^{-2T}}.
\]
In particular, there is $\upsilon$ large such that the map $\nu_T\longmapsto V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\nu_T)$ is not convex.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let us first verify the assumptions. 
	Note $c$ is bounded, affine, and thus convex in $\rho$, satisfies $c(x,\delta_x)=0$, and is jointly l.s.c.
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
	Moreover, $|b(t,x,\mu)|\le|x_t|+(\int|x'_t|^2\mu(t)({\mathrm{d}} x^\prime))^{1/2}$. 
	                                                                                                                               
	                                                                                          
	Note also that the coercivity condition holds.                                                                                      
\medskip


	In the remainder, given a rule $\gamma$ with deterministic flow and	$J(\gamma)<\infty$, we write $m_t\coloneqq\mathbb E[X_t]$, $\bar\alpha_t\coloneqq \mathbb E[\alpha_t]$, and $Z\coloneqq X-m$.
	To streamline the presentation, we drop the superscript $\gamma$. 
	Since $b(t,X,\mu_t)=m_t-X_t$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:decomp}
	m_t = \int_0^t\bar\alpha_r\,\mathrm dr,
	\; 
	{\mathrm{d}} Z_t
	=
	\big(\alpha_t-\bar\alpha_t-Z_t\big)\,\mathrm{d} t+{\mathrm{d}} W_t,
	\mbox{ and, }
	\mathbb{E} \bigg[ \int_0^T |\alpha_t|^2\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg] =\int_0^T\!|\bar\alpha_t|^2\mathrm dt +\mathbb{E}\bigg[  \int_0^T |\alpha_t-\bar\alpha_t|^2\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg],
\end{equation}
	where the first identity follows from the state equation and the cost decomposition follows from the variance formula.\medskip

	Let us first obtain the upper bounds.
	Let $k\in \mathbb{R}$, to be given, and put $\alpha_t^+ \coloneqq \frac \upsilon T+k (X_t-m_t)$. 
	Note that the McKean-Vlasov SDE with control $\alpha^+$ has a unique strong solution $X^+$, whose flow is deterministic, and thus induces an element of $\Gamma_{\rm det}(\nu_0)$. 
	By \eqref{eq:decomp}, $m_t^+ \coloneqq \mathbb{E}[X^+_T] = \frac \upsilon T  t$, hence $m_T= \upsilon $, and $Z^+ = X^+-m^+$ satisfies ${\mathrm{d}} Z_t^+=(k-1)Z_t^+ {\mathrm{d}} t+{\mathrm{d}} W_t$, so it is an Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process.
	Thus, $v_k(t)\coloneqq \mathbb E[Z_t^2]$ solves $v^\prime_k=2(k-1)v_k+1$, $v_k(0)=1$. 
	Note that $k\longmapsto v_k(T)$ is continuous with $v_k(T)\longrightarrow 0$ as $k\longrightarrow -\infty$ and $v_k(T)\longrightarrow\infty$ as $k\longrightarrow  +\infty$.
	Thus, there is $k=k_{\theta,T}$ with $v_k(T)=\theta ^2$. 
	Then, $X_T=m_T^+ +Z_T\sim\mathcal N(\upsilon ,\theta^2)=\nu_{T}^{+}$, and, using $\mathbb E[Z_t]=0$ and \eqref{eq:decomp},
	\[
		J(\gamma^+)
		=\frac12\mathbb{E}\bigg[ \int_0^T \big |\frac \upsilon T+k Z_t \big|^2 \,\mathrm{d} t \bigg]
		=
		\frac{\upsilon ^2}{2T}+\frac{k^2}{2}\int_0^T v(t)\,\mathrm{d}  t \eqqcolon \frac{\upsilon^2}{2T}+c_{\theta,T}.
	\]
	The fact that $J(\gamma^-)= \frac{\upsilon^2}{2T}+c_{\theta,T}$ follows similarly with $\alpha_t^- \coloneqq  -\frac \upsilon T+k (X_t-m_t)$.\medskip

	We now obtain the lower bound on $V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\bar{\nu}_T)$. 
	Let $\gamma\in\Gamma^{\rm det}_{c}(\nu_0,\bar{\nu}_T)$ with $J(\gamma)<\infty$, otherwise the result holds. 
	Note the terminal constraint implies $m_T=0$ and $\mathbb{E}[Z_T^2]=\mathrm{Var}(\bar{\nu}_T)=\upsilon^2+\theta^2$ and that we might write \eqref{eq:decomp} as
\[
Z_T=e^{-T}Z_0+\int_0^T e^{-(T-r)}\big(\alpha_r-\bar\alpha_r\big)\mathrm dr
+\int_0^T e^{-(T-r)}\,\mathrm dW_r .
\]
Moreover, by definition of $\nu_{0}$, $\mathbb{E}[ |Z_0|^2]=1$, and $\int_0^T\! {\mathrm{e}}^{-2(T-r)}{\mathrm{d}} r =(1-e^{-2T})/2\leq  1/2$.
	By Cauchy--Schwarz,
\[
\sqrt{\upsilon^2+\theta^2}
	 \leq {\mathrm{e}}^{-T} + \frac1{\sqrt2}
	+ \sqrt{\frac{1-e^{-2T}}{2}}
	\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg[  \int_0^T |\alpha_r-\bar\alpha_r|^2\,\mathrm{d} r\bigg] \bigg)^{1/2},
\]
and consequently, by \eqref{eq:decomp},
\[
J(\gamma)\geq 
	\dfrac12 \mathbb{E}\bigg [  \int_0^T |\alpha-\bar\alpha|^2 \,\mathrm{d} t\bigg]
	\geq
	C_{\upsilon,\theta,T}.
\]
Since $\gamma$ was arbitrary, this proves the lower bound $V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_0,\bar{\nu}_T)  \geq C_{\upsilon,\theta,T}$.\medskip

To conclude, note that since $1-{\mathrm{e}}^{-2T}< 1/(2T)$, for $\upsilon$ large enough, the leading coefficient of $\upsilon^2$ in $C_{\upsilon,\theta,T}$ exceeds that of the averaged upper bounds. That is
\[
V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_{0},\bar{\nu}_T )
	 >
	\dfrac12 V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}^{+})+\dfrac12V^{\rm det}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}^{-}).\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop.convex.V}
	Let $\nu_{0}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$. Then, ${\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ is convex and the mapping ${\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)\ni\nu_{T}\longmapsto V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ is convex.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let us first note that ${\cal P}(\nu_{0})$ is convex.
	Indeed, this follows from {\rm \cite[Theorem $1.(i)$]{djete2020mckean2}}, and \Cref{rmk.equiv}.
	Let $\theta \in (0,1)$, $\nu_{T}^i\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$, $i=1,2$ be given, and $\mathbb{P}^i \in {\cal P}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}^i)$ be given from $\gamma^i\in \Gamma(\nu_{0}^i,\nu_{T}^i)$ as in \Cref{rmk.equiv}.
	Let $\overline \mathbb{P} = \theta \mathbb{P}^1 + (1 - \theta)\mathbb{P}^2$ and define ${\overline \nu}_T$ similarly.
	We claim that $\overline \mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},{\overline\nu}_T)$.
	Since ${\cal P}(\nu_{0})$ is convex, it only remains to show                                   
	that $\overline \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T\=c{\overline\nu}_T$. 
	                                                                         
	Let us recall from \cite[Theorem 1.3]{backhoff2019existence} the duality formula for ${\cal W}_c$
	\begin{align}\label{eq.dualitywot}
		{\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T , \nu_{T}) = \sup_{\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^d)}(\langle Q_c\varphi,\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T\rangle -\langle \varphi,\nu_{T}\rangle  ).
	\end{align}
	Thus, since $\overline \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T=\theta \mathbb{P}^1\circ X^{-1}_T+(1-\theta) \mathbb{P}^2\circ X^{-1}_T$, and $c$ is nonnegative, $\overline \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T\=c{\overline\nu}_T$ follows since we have that 
	\[
	0\leq {\cal W}_c(\overline \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T,{\overline \nu}_T ) \le \theta {\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}^1\circ X^{-1}_T, \nu_{T}^1) + (1-\theta){\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}^2\circ X^{-1}_T, \nu_{T}^2 ) = 0.
	\]
	
	Moreover, we have
	\begin{align*}
		V_c({\overline\nu}_0,{\overline\nu}_T) \le {\cal J}(\overline \mathbb{P}) = \mathbb{E}^{\bar\mathbb{P}}\bigg[ \int_0^T f(t,X, u_t,\mu_t)\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg] 
		& = \theta \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^1}\bigg[ \int_0^T f(t,X, u_t,\mu_t)\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg] + (1 - \theta)\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^2}\bigg[ \int_0^T f(t,X, u_t, \mu_t )\,\mathrm{d} t\bigg]\\
		& = \theta  {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}^1) + (1 - \theta) {\cal J}( \mathbb{P}^2) .
	\end{align*}
	This yields the convexity of $\nu_{T}\longmapsto V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ since $\mathbb{P}^1$ and $\mathbb{P}^2$ were chosen arbitrarily.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:disint}
Let $\nu\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ and $\mathbb{P}\in{\cal P}(\nu)$.                                                                                                                          
	Then,                        
	$\mathbb{P}_\omega^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}\in{\cal P}_{\rm det}(\nu),\,\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$, and, $\bar\mu=\bar\mu(\omega), \, \mathbb{P}_\omega^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}\text{--a.s.}$ 
	Consequently,
	\(
	{\cal J}(\mathbb{P})=\langle {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}_\cdot^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}), \mathbb{P}\rangle,\;
	\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1} =\mathbb{P}\otimes (\mathbb{P}_\cdot^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}\circ X_T^{-1}).
	\)
                                                                                                                          
                                      
                                                                    
   
                                                        
                         
                
                                                               
In particular, for every $\varphi\in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, \(\Psi^\varphi(\nu)
=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\gamma \in \Gamma_{\rm det}(\nu)}J^\varphi(\gamma)\).
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Throughout we write $\xi_t:=(X_{t\wedge\cdot},U_{t\wedge\cdot},W)$ and $\mathbb F^\circ\coloneqq(\mathcal F^\circ_t)_{t\in[0,T]}$, $\mathcal F^\circ_t\coloneqq \sigma(X_{t\wedge\cdot},U_{t\wedge\cdot},W_{t\wedge\cdot})$, the filtration generated by the first three coordinates.
	Let us first verify that $\mathbb{P}_\omega$ satisfies \Cref{def.controlrule} for $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.e.} \, \omega\in \Omega$. We argue in four steps.                                                            
\medskip

{\bf Step 1}. There is $N\subseteq {\cal F}, \mathbb{P}(N)=0$, such that \(\bar\mu_t(\omega)=\mathbb P_\omega\circ\xi_t^{-1},\;\mbox{for all } t\in[0,T],\, \omega \in  N^c\). 
	Since $\bar\mu$ is $\overline{{\cal G}}_T$--measurable, there is a
$\mathbb P$--null set $N_1$ with $\bar\mu=\bar\mu(\omega)$,
$\mathbb P_\omega$--a.s., for $\omega\notin N_1$. 
	Next, let $t\in[0,T]$. 
	By \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.3} there is a $\mathbb{P}$--null set $N(t)$ such that $\bar\mu_t(\omega)=\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ\xi_t^{-1}$ for
$\omega\notin N(t)$. Let $N_2:=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb Q_T}N(t)$ with $\mathbb Q_T:=(\mathbb Q\cap[0,T])\cup\{0,T\}$, a $\mathbb{P}$--null set. 
	The statement holds with $N_1\cup N_2$ by continuity in time on both sides of $\bar\mu_t(\omega)=\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ\xi_t^{-1}$.
	\medskip
	
{\bf Step 2}. $\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ(X_0,W)^{-1} =\mathbb{P}\circ(X_0,W)^{-1}=\nu\otimes\gamma_{\rm w}$, $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$, where $\gamma_{\rm w}$ denotes the Wiener measure on ${\cal C}^d$. 
	In particular, $\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ X_0^{-1}=\nu$ and $\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ W_0^{-1}=\delta_0$.
	Since $(X_0,W)$ and $\overline{\mathcal G}_T$ are independent, for any bounded Borel $h$, $\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[h(X_0,W)]$ is constant and a version of $\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[h(X_0,W)|\overline{\mathcal G}_T]$.
	Taking $h$ in a countable measure-determining family and since $\omega\longmapsto\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ(X_0,W)^{-1}$ is a kernel, we get $\mathbb{P}_\omega\circ(X_0,W)^{-1} =\mathbb{P}\circ(X_0,W)^{-1}=\nu\otimes\gamma_{\rm w}$, $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$
	\medskip
	
{\bf Step 3}. $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$, $W$ is an $(\mathbb{F}^\circ,\mathbb{P}_\omega)$--Brownian motion and $\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_\omega}\big[\sup_t\|X_t\|^p
+\int_0^T\|u_t\|^p{\mathrm{d}} t \big]<+ \infty.$
	Note that the second statement follows from the tower property.
	Let $0\le s<r\le T$ be rationals, $\phi:\mathbb{R}^d\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$, $\varphi:\bar\Omega\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ be bounded continuous, and define the bounded Borel functions on $\bar\Omega$, 
	$ h(\bar{\mathbf x},\bar{\mathbf u},\bar{\mathbf w})\coloneqq \phi(\bar{\mathbf w}_r-\bar{\mathbf w}_s) \varphi(\bar{\mathbf x}_{s\wedge\cdot},\bar{\mathbf u}_{s\wedge\cdot},\bar{\mathbf w}_{s\wedge\cdot})$,
	$h_2(\bar{\mathbf x},\bar{\mathbf u},\bar{\mathbf w})\coloneqq \varphi(\bar{\mathbf x}_{s\wedge\cdot},\bar{\mathbf u}_{s\wedge\cdot},\bar{\mathbf w}_{s\wedge\cdot})$.
	Note that, since the third component of $\xi_s$ is the path $W$, $h(\xi_s)=\phi(W_r-W_s)\varphi(X_{s\wedge\cdot}, U_{s\wedge\cdot},W_{s\wedge\cdot})$. 
	We first show
	\begin{equation}\label{eq:condindepW}
	\langle h,\bar\mu_s\rangle
	=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}} [\phi(W_r-W_s)] \langle h_2,\bar\mu_s\rangle, \; \mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}
	\end{equation}
	Since both sides are $\overline{{\cal G}}_s$--measurable, it suffices to test against $\mathbf 1_G$, $G\in\overline{{\cal G}}_s$. 
	By \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.3}, $\mathbb{E}[\langle h,\bar\mu_s\rangle\mathbf 1_G] =\mathbb{E}[h(\xi_s)\mathbf 1_G] =\mathbb{E}[\phi(W_r-W_s) h_2(\xi_s)\mathbf 1_G]$. 
	Note $h_2(\xi_s)\mathbf 1_G$ is ${\cal F}_s$--measurable and $W_r-W_s$ is $\mathbb{P}$--independent of ${\cal F}_s$.
	Thus, $\mathbb{E}[\langle h,\bar\mu_s\rangle\mathbf 1_G] =\mathbb{E}[\phi(W_r-W_s)]  \mathbb{E}[h_2(\xi_s)\mathbf 1_G] =\mathbb{E}[\phi(W_r-W_s)] \mathbb{E}[\langle h_2,\bar\mu_s\rangle\mathbf 1_G]$, and since $\phi$ and $\varphi$ were arbitrary, we obtain \eqref{eq:condindepW}.
	\medskip
	
By {\bf Step 1} at time $s$, for $\omega\in N^c$, $\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_\omega}[h(\xi_s)]=\langle h,\bar\mu_s(\omega)\rangle$ and $\mathbb E^{\mathbb P_\omega}[h_2(\xi_s)]=\langle h_2,\bar\mu_s(\omega)\rangle$, so \eqref{eq:condindepW} gives that $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$,
	\[
	\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_\omega}[\phi(W_r-W_s) \varphi(X_{s\wedge\cdot},U_{s\wedge\cdot},W_{s\wedge\cdot})] 
	=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\big[\phi(W_r-W_s)\big] \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_\omega}\big[\varphi(X_{s\wedge\cdot},U_{s\wedge\cdot},W_{s\wedge\cdot})\big].
	\]
Taking $\varphi\equiv1$ shows that the law of $W_r-W_s$ under $\mathbb{P}_\omega$ agrees with that of $W_r-W_s$ under $\mathbb P$, i.e., ${\cal N}(0,(r-s)\mathrm I_d)$.
	Thus, the previous equation shows $W_r-W_s$ is $\mathbb{P}_\omega$--independent of ${\cal F}^\circ_s$. 
	Choosing countable separating families of $(\phi,\varphi)$ and intersecting the null sets over rational $s<r$, and subsequently extending to arbitrary $s<r$ by continuity of $W$ and dominated convergence, we conclude that $W$ has independent, stationary Gaussian increments with respect to $\mathbb F^\circ$ under $\mathbb P_\omega$, $\mathbb{P}\text{--a.s.}$ 
	Together with {\bf Step 2}, the claim follows.\medskip


{\bf Step 4}. Arguing as in \cite[Lemma 4.3$(ii)$]{djete2019mckean}, we may enlarge the space to $\widehat \Omega\coloneqq \Omega\times\Omega^\prime$, $\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\coloneqq \mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^\prime$, where $(\Omega^\prime,P^\prime)$ carries a Brownian motion independent of everything to account for degenerate $\sigma_r\sigma_r^\top$, such that, with $\mu_r=\mu_r(\omega)$ as in {\bf Step 1},
\[
X_t=X_0+\int_0^t\big(\alpha_r+b(r,X,\mu_r(\omega))\big)\,\mathrm dr
+\int_0^t\sigma_r\,\mathrm dW_r,\qquad t\in[0,T], \; \mathbb{P}_\omega\otimes\mathbb{P}^\prime\text{--a.s.}.
\]

Let $N$ collect all the null sets of {\bf Steps~1--4}.
	It follows that for $\omega\in N^c$, under $\mathbb{P}_\omega\otimes\mathbb{P}^\prime$ the process $X$ solves the controlled SDE driven by the Brownian motion $W$, with control $u$, deterministic flow $\mu(\omega)$, initial law $\nu$, and the
integrability in \Cref{def.controlrule}\ref{def.controlrule.1} holds.
	By It\^o's formula, $M^\phi$ is a continuous $(\mathbb{F}^\circ,\mathbb{P}_\omega\otimes\mathbb{P}^\prime)$--local martingale for every $\phi\in C^2_b(\mathbb{R}^{m_d})$, hence an $(\mathbb{F}^\circ,\mathbb{P}_\omega)$--local martingale, since $M^\phi$ does
not involve the $\Omega^\prime$--coordinate, and ultimately an $(\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P}_\omega)$--local martingale, because every set in ${\cal F}_t$ coincides, up to a $\mathbb{P}_\omega$--null set, with a set in ${\cal F}^\circ_t$ since $\bar\mu$ is $\mathbb{P}_\omega\text{--a.s.}$ constant.  
	We conclude that $\mathbb{P}_\omega\in{\cal P}_{\rm det}(\nu)$.\medskip

The two equalities
	\(
	{\cal J}(\mathbb{P})=\langle {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}_\cdot^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}), \mathbb{P}\rangle
	\),
	and,
	\(
	\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1} =\mathbb{P}\otimes (\mathbb{P}_\cdot^{\overline{{\cal G}}_T}\circ X_T^{-1})
	\),
	follow from the tower property.
	Let us also note that \(\Psi^\varphi(\nu)
=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\gamma \in \Gamma_{\rm det}(\nu)}J^\varphi(\gamma)\) follows from these two equalities together with the inclusion $\mathcal P_{\rm det}(\nu)\subseteq\mathcal P(\nu)$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
For any $\nu_{0},\nu_{T}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$, with $\Psi^\varphi$ defined in the statement of \Cref{thm:duality.gen}, let us put
	\begin{equation*}
			D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \coloneqq \sup_{\varphi\in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)}\big(  \Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0}) -  \langle \varphi, \nu_{T}\rangle   \big).
	\end{equation*}
               
                   
                                                                                                                                                                                                   
                  

Assume $V_c(\nu_{0}, \nu_{T}) = + \infty$ for all $\nu_{T} \in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$.
	In this case, 
	 since
	 \begin{equation*}
		D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \ge  \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})} \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P}\bigg[\int_0^Tf(s, X, u_s,\mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s +   Q_c\varphi(X_T)\bigg] - \langle \varphi, \nu_{T}\rangle,
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\varphi\in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, 
	it follows from the convex dual representations of ${\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1},\nu_{T})$, namely \eqref{eq.dualitywot}, that $D(\nu_{0}, \nu_{T})\ge {\cal J}(\mathbb{P})  = \infty$. 
	In particular, $V_c(\nu_{0}, \nu_{T}) = D(\nu_{0}, \nu_{T})$.\medskip


	The rest of the proof of the convex dual representation will be done in two steps:\medskip

	{\bf Step 1.} \emph{Weak duality}.
	Let $\nu_{T}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ be such that $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})<\infty$.
	Then, for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$ such that
	\begin{align*}
		D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) &\leq \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\int_0^Tf(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s + Q_c\varphi(X_T) \bigg]  + \varepsilon 
	\end{align*}
	for any $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.
	Thus, we have 
	\begin{align*}
		D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) &\le \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P}\bigg[\int_0^Tf(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s \bigg] + \langle Q_c\varphi, \mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T\rangle - \langle \varphi, \nu_{T}\rangle  + \varepsilon\\
		 &\le {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}) +	\sup_{\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}Q_c\varphi(x)\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T({\mathrm{d}} x) - \langle \varphi, \nu_{T}\rangle \bigg)   + \varepsilon\\
		&={\cal J}(\mathbb{P})  + \varepsilon .
	\end{align*}
	Thus it follows that $D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \le {\cal J}(\mathbb{P}) + \varepsilon$.
		Given that $\mathbb{P} \in {\cal P}(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ and $\varepsilon>0$ were chosen arbitrarily, we deduce that $D(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) \le V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$.



	\medskip

	{\bf Step 2.} \emph{Strong duality}.
	Let again $\nu_{T}\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ be such that $V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})<\infty$.
	Thanks to Lemma \ref{Lem:v-LSC} and Proposition \ref{prop.convex.V}, since the topology on ${\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ is stronger than ${\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$, the function ${\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)\ni \nu_{T} \longmapsto V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})$ is convex and lower semicontinuous.
	{Denote by ${\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ the space of Borel signed measures on $\mathbb{R}^m$ with finite $p$ moment}, and equipped with the weak topology.
	                                      
	Notice that ${\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ and $C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$ are in separating duality and thus $\sigma({\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m), C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m))$ is a locally convex space whose dual is $\sigma(C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m),{\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m))$, see \cite[Theorem 3.10]{rudin1991functional}.
	We extend the function $V(\nu_{0},\cdot)$ to be $+\infty$ outside ${\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$, and still denote by $V$ this extension.
	It is standard that the function $V(\nu_{0},\cdot)$ remains convex and lower semicontinuous on $\sigma({\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m), C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m))$.
	It is also proper by the assumption above.
	                                                                                                                                                    
	Thus, by the Fenchel-Moreau theorem, see \cite[Theorem 2.3.3]{zalinescu2002convex}, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) = \sup_{\varphi \in C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m)}(\langle \varphi, \nu_{T} \rangle - V^\ast_c(\varphi)), \; \mbox{ where, } V^\ast_c(\varphi) \coloneqq \sup_{\nu_{T} \in {\cal M}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)}(\langle \varphi, \nu_{T}\rangle - V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T})),\; \varphi \in C_{p}(\mathbb{R}^m),
	\end{equation*}
	is the convex conjugate of $V_c$.
	Since $V^\ast_c$ is monotone, a monotone convergence argument gives
	\begin{equation*}
		V_c(\nu_{0},\nu_{T}) = \sup_{\varphi \in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)}(\langle \varphi, \nu_{T} \rangle - V^\ast_c(\varphi)).
	\end{equation*}	
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
                                                                                                                                
	                                                           
	                  
	                           
	                                                                                                                        
	                

	To conclude the proof, we compute the convex conjugate $V^\ast_c$.
	We will show that $V^*(-\varphi) = -\Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})$ for all $\varphi\in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$                                                  
	which will give the result. 
	We first show $-V^\ast(-\varphi)\geq \Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})$. 
	By definition of $V^\ast_c$, we have
	\begin{align}\label{eq.rules.det.random}
		-V^\ast_c(-\varphi) &= -\sup_{\eta \in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)}  \big(-\langle \varphi,\eta\rangle -V_c(\nu_{0},\eta)\big)\notag \\                                                                                 
		&=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\eta \in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)} \bigg(\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\eta)} \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P} \bigg[ \int_0^Tf(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s\bigg]+\langle \varphi,\eta\rangle\bigg)\notag \\
		&=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\eta\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)} \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\eta)} \bigg( \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P} \bigg[ \int_0^Tf(s,X,u_s,\mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s \bigg]+\langle \varphi,\eta \rangle\bigg).
\end{align}
Now, we recall that by definition $\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\mu)$ means $\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}(\nu_{0})$ and $0={\cal W}_c(\mathbb{P}_T,\mu) \ge \langle Q_c\varphi,\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T\rangle -   \langle\varphi,\mu\rangle$ for all $\varphi\in C_{b,p}(\mathbb{R}^m)$. 
                                                                  
Then, we have
\begin{align*}
-V^\ast_c(-\varphi)&\ge \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mu\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)} \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\mu)} \bigg( \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P} \bigg[ \int_0^T f(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s\bigg]+\langle Q_c\varphi,\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}_T \rangle\bigg)\\
                                                                                                                                                             
&= \mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbb{P}\in{\cal P}(\nu_{0})} \mathbb{E}^\mathbb{P} \bigg[ \int_0^T f(s, X, u_s, \mu_s)\,\mathrm{d} s + Q_c\varphi(X_T)\bigg] = \Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})
\end{align*}
where in the first equality, we used the fact that, thanks to \Cref{assump.costs}, we have
\[
{\cal P}(\nu_{0})=\bigcup_{\mu\in{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)} {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\mu).
\]
We now argue that $-V^\ast(-\varphi)\leq \Psi^\varphi(\nu_{0})$. 
To this end, some definitions and notation are needed.
For any probability measure $P\in {\cal P}({\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m))$, recall that the intensity $I(P)\in{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$ of $P$ is the measure defined as
                              
\[
I(P)(A) :=\int_{{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)} p(A)P({\mathrm{d}} p),\quad \text{for a Borel set }A \subseteq \mathbb{R}^m.
\]
Similarly, the intensity of a measure $\tilde P\in  {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m \times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m))$ is the measure $\hat I(\tilde P)\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m \times\mathbb{R}^m)$ defined as
                                               
\[                                         
\hat I(\tilde P) (A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^m \times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)} \int_{ {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)}    \mathbf{1}_A(x,y )p({\mathrm{d}} y) \tilde P({\mathrm{d}} x,{\mathrm{d}} p) \quad\text{for a Borel set } A \subseteq \mathbb{R}^m \times \mathbb{R}^m.\]
For $\pi\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m \times{\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m))$, let ${\rm Proj}_\bullet (\pi)$ denote the projection onto the $\bullet$-coordinate, e.g., ${\rm Proj}_2 (\pi)\in{\cal P}({\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m))$ is given by ${\rm Proj}_2 (\pi)({\mathrm{d}} p)= \pi(\mathbb{R}^m\times {\mathrm{d}} p )$.
For any $\mu,\nu\in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m)$ we define
 $$\Lambda(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq \big\{\tilde P \in {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m \times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)) : {\rm Proj}_1 \tilde P=\mu,\, I({\rm Proj}_2(\tilde P))=\nu\big\}.$$

 \medskip

Let $\varepsilon>0$, and recall that by \cite[Proposition 7.47]{bertsekas1978stochastic} the function $Q_c \varphi$ is lower semianalytic.
Thus, it follows from \cite[Proposition 7.50]{bertsekas1978stochastic} that there is an analytically measurable kernel $(\pi_x)_{x\in \mathbb{R}^m}$, such that for all $x\in\mathbb{R}^m$, $\pi_x \in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ and, 
\begin{align}\label{eq.ms}
\langle \varphi,\pi_x\rangle +c(x,\pi_x)\leq Q_c\varphi(x)+\varepsilon.
\end{align}
Let $\nu\in {\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m)$ and $\mathbb{P}\in {\cal P}_c(\nu_{0},\nu)$. 
	The measure $\mathbb{P}\in {\rm Prob}(\Omega)$                     
	and $(\pi_x)_{x\in \mathbb{R}^m}$ define the measure $\tilde P^{\mathbb{P}}$ in $ {\cal P}(\mathbb{R}^m \times{\cal P}_p(\mathbb{R}^m))$
                                                                                       
by $\tilde P^{\mathbb{P}} ({\mathrm{d}} x,{\mathrm{d}} p) \coloneqq \delta_{\pi^x}({\mathrm{d}} p)\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1} ({\mathrm{d}} x)$.
	                                                             
                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
Its intensity and projections are respectively given by $\hat I(\tilde P^{\mathbb{P}})({\mathrm{d}} x, {\mathrm{d}} y) =\pi_x({\mathrm{d}} y)\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1} ({\mathrm{d}} x)$,
	                                                                    
	${\rm Proj}_1 (\hat I(\tilde P^{\mathbb{P}} ) ) (A)=\mathbb{P}\circ X_T^{-1} (A )$,
	and,
\[
